# Economic history of the United Kingdom

Economic history of the United Kingdom

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/GDP_in_England_since_1270%2C_OWiD%2C_log_scale.svg/960px-GDP_in_England_since_1270%2C_OWiD%2C_log_scale.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:GDP_in_England_since_1270,_OWiD,_log_scale.svg) [Gross domestic product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_domestic_product "Gross domestic product") (GDP) in England 1270 to 2016[&#8202;](#ref-back-1-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-1)

The **economic history of the United Kingdom** covers the [economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_development "Economic development") of the British state from its initial formation in 1707 following the [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union "Treaty of Union") and [Acts of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1707 "Acts of Union 1707") that resulted in the [political union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_union "Political union") of the kingdoms of [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England "Kingdom of England") and [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland "Kingdom of Scotland"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-2-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-2) into a new unitary state called [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain "Kingdom of Great Britain").[&#8202;](#ref-back-3-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-3) [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ireland "Ireland") was incorporated in the [United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland "United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland") economy between 1800 and 1922; from 1922 the [Irish Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Free_State "Irish Free State") (the modern [Republic of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Ireland "Republic of Ireland")) became independent and set its own economic policy.

[Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain "Great Britain"), and England in particular, became one of the most prosperous economic regions in the world between the late 1600s and early 1800s as a result of being the birthplace of the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution "Industrial Revolution") that began in the mid-eighteenth century.[&#8202;](#ref-back-4-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-4) The developments brought by [industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation "Industrialisation") resulted in Britain becoming the premier European and global economic, political, and military power for more than a century. As the first to industrialise, Britain's industrialists revolutionised areas like manufacturing, communication, and transportation through innovations such as the steam engine (for pumps, factories, railway locomotives and steamships), textile equipment, tool-making, the Telegraph, and pioneered the [railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway "Railway") system. With these many new technologies Britain manufactured much of the equipment and products used by other nations, becoming known as the "workshop of the world". Its businessmen were leaders in international commerce and banking, trade and shipping. Its markets included both areas that were independent and those that were part of the rapidly expanding [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire"), which by the early 1900s had become the largest empire in history. After 1840, the economic policy of [mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism "Mercantilism") was abandoned and replaced by [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade "Free trade"), with fewer tariffs, quotas or restrictions, first outlined by British economist Adam Smith's _[Wealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_of_Nations "Wealth of Nations")_. Britain's globally dominant [Royal Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Navy "Royal Navy") protected British commercial interests, shipping and international trade, while the British legal system provided a system for resolving disputes relatively inexpensively, and the [City of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_London "City of London") functioned as the economic capital and focus of the world economy.

Between 1870 and 1900, [economic output per head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_per_capita "Income per capita") of the United Kingdom rose by 50 per cent (from about £28 per capita to £41 in 1900: an annual average increase in real incomes of 1% p.a.), growth which was associated with a significant rise in [living standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_standards "Living standards").[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-5) However, and despite this significant economic growth, some economic historians have suggested that Britain experienced a _relative_ economic decline in the last third of the nineteenth century as industrial expansion occurred in the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_United_States "Economic history of the United States") and [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Germany "Economic history of Germany"). In 1870, Britain's output per head was the second highest in the world, surpassed only by [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Australia "Economic history of Australia"). In 1914, British income per capita was the world's third highest, exceeded only by [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_History_of_New_Zealand "Economic History of New Zealand") and Australia; these three countries shared a common economic, social and cultural heritage. In 1950, British output per head was still 30 per cent over that of the average of the six founder members of the [EEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EEC "EEC"), but within 20 years it had been overtaken by the majority of western European economies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-7)

The response of successive British governments to this problematic performance was to seek economic growth stimuli within what became the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union"); Britain entered the [European Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Community "European Community") in 1973. Thereafter the United Kingdom's relative economic performance improved substantially to the extent that, just before the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession "Great Recession"), British income per capita exceeded, albeit marginally, that of [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_France "Economic history of France") and Germany; furthermore, there was a significant reduction in the gap in income per capita terms between the UK and USA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Crafts-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-Crafts)

## 16th–17th centuries

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Arms_of_Owen_Tudor.svg/330px-Arms_of_Owen_Tudor.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arms_of_Owen_Tudor.svg) Ancient coat of arms of the Tudor family

Main article: [Commercial revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_revolution "Commercial revolution")

During the 16th and 17th century many fundamental economic changes occurred, these resulted in rising incomes and paved the way to the industrialisation. After 1600, the [North Sea Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea_Region "North Sea Region") took over the role of the leading economic centre of Europe from the Mediterranean, which prior to this date, particularly in [northern Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Italy "Northern Italy"), had been the most highly developed part of Europe. Great Britain, together with the [Low Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Countries "Low Countries"), profited more in the long run from the expansion of trade in the Atlantic and Asia than the pioneers of this trade, Spain and Portugal, fundamentally because of the success of the mainly privately owned enterprises in these two Northern countries in contrast to the arguably less successful state-owned economic systems in Iberia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-9)

After the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death "Black Death") in the mid 14th century, and the agricultural depression of the late 15th century, the population began to increase. The export of woollen products resulted in an economic upturn with products exported to mainland Europe. [Henry VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VII_of_England "Henry VII of England") negotiated the favourable [Intercursus Magnus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercursus_Magnus "Intercursus Magnus") treaty in 1496.[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-10)

The high wages and abundance of available land seen in the late 15th century and early 16th century were temporary. When the population recovered, low wages and a land shortage returned. Historians in the early 20th century characterised the economic in terms of general decline, manorial reorganisation, and agricultural contraction. Later historians dropped those themes and stressed the transitions between medieval forms and Tudor progress.[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-11)

[John Leland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Leland_(antiquary) "John Leland (antiquary)") left rich descriptions of the local economies he witnessed during his travels from 1531 to 1560. He described markets, ports, industries, buildings and transport links. He showed some small towns were expanding, through new commercial and industrial opportunities, especially cloth manufacture. He found other towns in decline, and suggested that investment by entrepreneurs and benefactors had enabled some small towns to prosper.[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-12) Taxation was a negative factor in economic growth, since it was imposed, not on consumption, but on capital investments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-13)

According to [Derek Hirst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derek_Hirst "Derek Hirst"), outside of politics and religion, the 1640s and 1650s saw a revived economy characterised by growth in manufacturing, the elaboration of financial and credit instruments, and the commercialisation of communication. The gentry found time for leisure activities, such as horse racing and bowling. In the high culture important innovations included the development of a mass market for music, increased scientific research, and an expansion of publishing. All the trends were discussed in depth at the newly established coffee houses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-14)

### Credit and money supply

See also: [The Great Debasement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_Debasement "The Great Debasement") and [Financial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Revolution "Financial Revolution")

The economy grew faster than the money supply in this period. Few coins were in circulation due to the scarcity of precious metals and instead most transactions were made on credit. The numerous debts were usually settled during _reckonings_ held at convenient intervals. Reciprocal debts were cancelled and only the difference had to be paid with cash. Other than that coins were used for specific purposes such as small transactions between strangers, for paying taxes and for overseas trade. Overall coins formed less than 10% of all exchanges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-15)

Spanish and Portuguese colonies in the New World exported large quantities of silver and gold to Europe, some of which added to the English money supply. There were multiple results that all expanded the English economy, according to Dr. Nuno Palma of the University of Manchester. The key features of the growth pattern included specialisation and structural change, and increases in market participation. The new supply of specie (silver and gold) increased the money supply. Instead of promissory notes paid off by future promissory notes, business transactions were supported by specie. This reduced transaction costs, increased the coverage of the market, and opened incentives and opportunities to participate in cash transaction. Demand arose for luxury goods from Asia, such as silk and pepper, which created new markets. The increased supply of specie made tax collection easier, allowing the government to build up fiscal capacity and provide for public goods.[&#8202;](#ref-back-16-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-16)

Various inflationary pressures existed; some were due to an influx of New World gold and a rising population. Inflation had a negative effect on the real wealth of most families.[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-17) It set the stage for social upheaval as the gap between the rich and poor widening. This was a period of significant change for the majority of the rural population, with [manorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manorial "Manorial") land owners introducing [enclosure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enclosure "Enclosure") measures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-18)

### Exports

See also: [Maritime history of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_history_of_the_United_Kingdom "Maritime history of the United Kingdom")

Exports increased significantly, especially within the British empire. Mostly privately owned companies traded with the colonies in the [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_West_Indies "British West Indies"), [Northern America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America "British North America") and [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidencies_and_provinces_of_British_India "Presidencies and provinces of British India").[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-19)

The [Company of Merchant Adventurers of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_of_Merchant_Adventurers_of_London "Company of Merchant Adventurers of London") brought together [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_London "City of London")'s leading overseas merchants in a regulated company in the early 15th century, in the nature of a [guild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guild "Guild"). Its members' main business was the export of cloth, especially white (undyed) woollen [broadcloth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcloth "Broadcloth"). This enabled them to import a large range of foreign goods.[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-20)[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-21) Given the trade conditions of the time, England came up with a public policy to increase [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenia "Armenia") merchants' involvement in English trade to pursue its commercial interests. Accordingly, in 1688 a trade agreement was signed in London between [English East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_East_India_Company "English East India Company") and Kjoja Panos Kalantar. [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenia "Armenia") merchants received privileges to carry out commercial activities in England. England, in its turn, wanted to change the main trade route from Europe to Asia, and from now on, [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenia "Armenia"), instead of overlanding in Arabia or Persia, used England. With this agreement, England wanted to take over the control that Dutch and France had at that time on world exports.[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-22)

### Wool industry

Further information: [Medieval English wool trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_English_wool_trade "Medieval English wool trade")

Woollen cloth was the chief export and most important employer after agriculture. The golden era of the Wiltshire woollen industry was in the reign of Henry VIII. In the medieval period, raw wool had been exported, but now England had an industry, based on its 11 million sheep. London and towns purchased wool from dealers, and send it to rural households where family labour turned it into cloth. They washed the wool, carded it and spun it into thread, which was then turned into cloth on a loom. Export merchants, known as [Merchant Adventurers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_of_Merchant_Adventurers_of_London "Company of Merchant Adventurers of London"), exported woollens into the Netherlands and Germany, as well as other lands. The arrival of [Huguenots from France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Huguenot "French Huguenot") brought in new skills that expanded the industry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-23)[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-24)[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-25)

Government intervention proved a disaster in the early 17th century. A new company convinced Parliament to transfer to them the monopoly held by the old, well-established [Company of Merchant Adventurers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_of_Merchant_Adventurers_of_London "Company of Merchant Adventurers of London"). Arguing that the export of unfinished cloth was much less profitable than the export of the finished product, the new company got Parliament to ban the export of unfinished cloth. There was massive dislocation marketplace, as large unsold quantities built up, prices fell, and unemployment rose. Worst of all, the Dutch retaliated and refused to import any finished cloth from England. Exports fell by a third. Quickly the ban was lifted, and the Merchant Adventurers got its monopoly back. However, the trade losses became permanent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-26)

### Diets

Diet depended largely upon social class. The rich ate meat—beef, pork, venison and white bread; the poor ate coarse dark bread, with a little meat, notably at Christmas. [Game poaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_poaching "Game poaching") supplemented the diet of the rural poor. Everyone drank [ale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ale "Ale") or [cider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cider "Cider") as water was often too impure to drink. [Fruits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruit "Fruit") and [vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetable "Vegetable") were seldom eaten. Rich spices were used by the wealthy to offset the smells of stale [salted meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salted_meat "Salted meat"). The [potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato "Potato") had yet to become part of the diet. The rich enjoyed desserts such as [pastries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastries "Pastries"), [tarts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tart "Tart"), [cakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cake "Cake"), and crystallised fruit, and syrup.[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-27)

Among the rich private hospitality was an important item in their budget. Entertaining a royal party for a few weeks could be ruinous to a nobleman. Inns existed for travellers but restaurants were not known.

Both the rich and the poor had diets with nutritional deficiency. The lack of vegetables and fruits in their diets caused a deficiency in vitamin C, sometimes resulting in [scurvy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scurvy "Scurvy").

Trade and industry flourished in the 16th century, making England more prosperous and improving the standard of living of the upper and middle classes. However, the lower classes did not benefit much and did not always have enough food. As the English population was fed by its own agricultural produce, a series of bad harvests in the 1590s caused widespread distress.[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-28)

In the 17th century the food supply improved. England had no food crises from 1650 to 1725, a period when France was unusually vulnerable to famines. Historians point out that oat and barley prices in England did not always increase following a failure of the wheat crop, but did so in France.[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-29)

### Poverty

Further information: [Poor relief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_relief "Poor relief")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f8/Vagrant_being_punished_in_the_streets_%28Tudor_England%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vagrant_being_punished_in_the_streets_(Tudor_England).jpg) A woodcut from circa 1536 depicting a vagrant being punished in the streets in Tudor England

About one-third of the population lived in poverty, with the wealthy expected to give [alms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alms "Alms") to assist the [impotent poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impotent_poor "Impotent poor").[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-30) Tudor law was harsh on the [able-bodied poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Able-bodied_poor "Able-bodied poor"), those physically fit but unable to find work. Those who left their [parishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parishes "Parishes") in order to locate work were termed [vagabonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vagabonds "Vagabonds") and could be subjected to punishments, often suffering whipping and being put in the stocks. This treatment was inflicted to encourage them to return to their "mother parish."[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-31)[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-32)

## 18th century

### The trading nation

The 18th century was prosperous as entrepreneurs extended the range of their business around the globe. By the 1720s Britain was one of the most prosperous countries in the world, and [Daniel Defoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Defoe "Daniel Defoe") boasted:
"We are the most diligent nation in the world. Vast trade, rich manufactures, mighty wealth, universal correspondence, and happy success have been constant companions of England, and given us the title of an industrious people."[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-33)

While the other major powers were primarily motivated toward territorial gains, and protection of their dynasties (such as the Habsburg, Romanov dynasty and Prussia's [House of Hohenzollern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Hohenzollern "House of Hohenzollern")), Britain had a different set of primary interests. Its main diplomatic goal (besides protecting the homeland from invasion) was building a worldwide trading network for its merchants, manufacturers, shippers and financiers. This required a hegemonic [Royal Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Navy "Royal Navy") so powerful that no rival could sweep its ships from the world's trading routes, or invade the British Isles. The London government enhanced the [private sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector "Private sector") by incorporating numerous privately financed London-based companies for establishing trading posts and opening import-export businesses across the world. Each was given a monopoly of trade to the specified geographical region. The first enterprise was the [Muscovy Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscovy_Company "Muscovy Company") set up in 1555 to trade with Russia. Other prominent enterprises included the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company "East India Company"), and the [Hudson's Bay Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson's_Bay_Company "Hudson's Bay Company") in Canada. The Company of Royal Adventurers Trading to Africa had been set up in 1662 to trade in gold, ivory and slaves in Africa; it was reestablished as the [Royal African Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_African_Company "Royal African Company") in 1672 and focused on the [Atlantic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade "Atlantic slave trade"). British involvement in each of the four major wars, 1740 to 1783, paid off handsomely in terms of trade. Even the loss of the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies "Thirteen Colonies") was made up by a very favourable trading relationship with the new United States of America. Britain gained dominance in the trade with India, and largely dominated the highly lucrative slave, sugar, and commercial trades originating in West Africa and the West Indies. Exports soared from £6.5 million in 1700, to £14.7 million in 1760 and £43.2 million in 1800.[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-34) Other powers set up similar monopolies on a much smaller scale; only the Netherlands emphasised trade as much as England.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-35)[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-36)

Most of the companies earned good profits, and enormous personal fortunes were created in India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-37) However, there was one major fiasco that caused heavy losses. The [South Sea Bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sea_Bubble "South Sea Bubble") was a business enterprise that exploded in scandal. The [South Sea Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sea_Company "South Sea Company") was a private business corporation supposedly set up much like the other trading companies, with a focus on [South America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_America "South America"). Its actual purpose was to renegotiate previous high-interest government loans amounting to £31 million through market manipulation and speculation. It issued stock four times in 1720 that reached about 8,000 investors. Prices kept soaring every day, from £130 a share to £1,000, with insiders making huge paper profits. The Bubble collapsed overnight, ruining many speculators. Investigations showed bribes had reached into high places—even to the king. His chief minister [Robert Walpole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Walpole "Robert Walpole") managed to wind it down with minimal political and economic damage, although some losers fled to exile or committed suicide.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-38)[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-39)

### The age of mercantilism

Main article: [Mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism "Mercantilism")

The basis of the British Empire was founded in the age of [mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism "Mercantilism"), an economic theory that stressed maximising the exports to and minimising imports from countries outside the empire, and trying to weaken rival empires. The 18th century British Empire was based upon the preceding [English overseas possessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_overseas_possessions "English overseas possessions"), which began to take shape in the late 16th and early 17th century, with the English settlement of islands of the [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies "West Indies") such as [Trinidad and Tobago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinidad_and_Tobago "Trinidad and Tobago"), [the Bahamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bahamas "The Bahamas"), the [Leeward Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeward_Islands "Leeward Islands"), [Barbados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbados "Barbados"), [Jamaica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaica "Jamaica"), and [Bermuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bermuda "Bermuda"), and of Virginia, one of the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies "Thirteen Colonies") which in 1776 became the United States, as well as of the [Maritime provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_provinces "Maritime provinces") of what is now Canada. The [sugar plantation islands of the Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_plantations_in_the_Caribbean "Sugar plantations in the Caribbean"), where slavery became the basis of the economy, comprised England's most lucrative colonies. The American colonies also used [slave labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_colonial_history_of_the_United_States "Slavery in the colonial history of the United States") in the farming of tobacco, indigo, and rice in the south. England, and later Great Britain's, American empire was slowly expanded by war and colonisation. Victory over the French during the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War "Seven Years' War") gave Great Britain control over what is now [eastern Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Canada "Eastern Canada").[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-40)

Mercantilism was the basic policy imposed by Britain on its colonies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-41) Mercantilism meant that the government and the merchants became partners with the goal of increasing political power and private wealth, to the exclusion of other empires. The government protected its merchants—and kept others out—by trade barriers, regulations, and subsidies to domestic industries in order to maximise exports from and minimise imports to the realm. The [Navigation Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navigation_Acts "Navigation Acts") of the late 17th century provided the legal foundation for Mercantilist policy. They required all trade to be carried in English ships, manned by English crews (this later encompassed all Britons after the [Acts of Union 1707](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1707 "Acts of Union 1707") united Scotland with England).[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-42) Colonists were required to send their produce and raw materials first of all to Britain, where the surplus was then sold-on by British merchants to other colonies in the British empire or bullion-earning external markets. The colonies were forbidden to trade directly with other nations or rival empires. The goal was to maintain the North American and Caribbean colonies as dependent agricultural economies geared towards producing raw materials for export to Britain. The growth of native industry was discouraged, in order to keep the colonies dependent on Britain for their finished goods.[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-43)

The government had to fight smuggling—which became a favorite American technique in the 18th century to circumvent the restrictions on trading with the French, Spanish or Dutch.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-44) The goal of mercantilism was to run trade surpluses, so that gold and silver would pour into London. The government took its share through duties and taxes, with the remainder going to merchants in Britain. The government spent much of its revenue on a superb [Royal Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Navy "Royal Navy"), which not only protected the British colonies but threatened the colonies of the other empires, and sometimes seized them. Thus the British Navy captured [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam "New Amsterdam") ( [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City "New York City")) in 1664. The colonies were captive markets for British industry, and the goal was to enrich the mother country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-45)

### Manufacturing

Besides woollens, cotton, silk and linen cloth manufacturing became important after 1600, as did coal and iron.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-46)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Philipp_Jakob_Loutherbourg_d._J._002.jpg/500px-Philipp_Jakob_Loutherbourg_d._J._002.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Philipp_Jakob_Loutherbourg_d._J._002.jpg) _[Coalbrookdale by Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalbrookdale_by_Night "Coalbrookdale by Night")_, 1801\. Blast furnaces light the iron making town of [Coalbrookdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalbrookdale "Coalbrookdale").

In 1709, [Abraham Darby I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Darby_I "Abraham Darby I") established a [coke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coke_(fuel) "Coke (fuel)")-fired blast furnace to produce cast iron, replacing charcoal, although continuing to use blast furnaces. The ensuing availability of inexpensive iron was one of the factors leading to the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution "Industrial Revolution"). Toward the end of the 18th century, cast iron began to replace wrought iron for certain purposes, because it was cheaper. Carbon content in iron was not implicated as the reason for the differences in properties of [wrought iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrought_iron "Wrought iron"), [cast iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cast_iron "Cast iron"), and [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel "Steel") until the 18th century.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-47)

### The Industrial Revolution

Main article: [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution "Industrial Revolution")

See also: [Industrial Revolution in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution_in_Scotland "Industrial Revolution in Scotland") and [Industrial Revolution in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution_in_Wales "Industrial Revolution in Wales")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Map_of_Northern_England_with_settlements_and_traditional_counties.png/500px-Map_of_Northern_England_with_settlements_and_traditional_counties.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Map_of_Northern_England_with_settlements_and_traditional_counties.png) The industrial revolution led to mass [urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation "Urbanisation"), especially in Northern England

In period loosely dated from the 1770s to the 1820s, Britain experienced an accelerated process of economic change that transformed a largely agrarian economy into the world's first industrial economy. This phenomenon is known as the " [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution "Industrial Revolution")", since the changes were far-reaching and permanent throughout many areas of Britain, especially in the developing cities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-48)

Economic, institutional, and social changes were fundamental to the emergence of the industrial revolution. Whereas absolutism remained the normal form of governance through most parts of Europe, in the UK a fundamentally different power balance was created after the revolutions of 1640 and 1688. The new institutional setup ensured property rights and political safety and thereby supported the emergence of an economically prosperous middle class. Another factor is the change in marriage patterns through this period. Marrying later allowed young people to acquire more education, thereby building up more human capital in the population. These changes enhanced the already relatively developed labour and financial markets, paving the way for the industrial revolution starting in the mid-18th century.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-49)

Great Britain provided the legal and cultural foundations that enabled entrepreneurs to pioneer the industrial revolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-50) Starting in the later part of the 18th century, there began a transition in parts of Great Britain's previously manual labour and draft-animal–based economy towards machine-based manufacturing. It started with the mechanisation of the textile industries, the development of [iron-making](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron-making "Iron-making") techniques and the increased use of [refined coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coke_(fuel) "Coke (fuel)"). Trade expansion was enabled by the introduction of [canals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal "Canal"), [improved roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turnpike_trusts "Turnpike trusts") and [railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railways "Railways"). Factories pulled thousands from low productivity work in agriculture to high productivity urban jobs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-51)

The introduction of [steam power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_power "Steam power") fuelled primarily by coal, wider utilisation of [water wheels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_wheel "Water wheel") and powered machinery (mainly in [textile manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_manufacturing "Textile manufacturing")) underpinned the dramatic increases in production capacity. The development of all-metal [machine tools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_tool "Machine tool") in the first two decades of the 19th century facilitated the manufacture of more production machines for manufacturing in other industries. The effects spread throughout Western Europe and North America during the 19th century, eventually affecting most of the world, a process that continues as [industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation "Industrialisation"). The historian [Emma Griffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emma_Griffin "Emma Griffin") has placed particular emphasis on the role of the steam engine in the making of Britain's Industrial Revolution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-52)

According to [Max Weber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Weber "Max Weber"), the foundations of this process of change can be traced back to the Puritan Ethic of the [Puritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puritans "Puritans") of the 17th century.[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-53) This produced modern personalities attuned to innovation and committed to a work ethic, inspiring landed and merchant elites alive to the benefits of modernisation, and a system of agriculture able to produce increasingly cheap food supplies. To this must be added the influence of religious nonconformity, which increased literacy and inculcated a " [Protestant work ethic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_work_ethic "Protestant work ethic")" amongst skilled artisans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-54)

A long run of good harvests, starting in the first half of the 18th century, resulted in an increase in disposable income and a consequent rising demand for manufactured goods, particularly textiles. The invention of the [flying shuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_shuttle "Flying shuttle") by [John Kay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kay_(flying_shuttle) "John Kay (flying shuttle)") enabled wider cloth to be woven faster, but also created a demand for yarn that could not be fulfilled. Thus, the major technological advances associated with the industrial revolution were concerned with spinning. [James Hargreaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Hargreaves "James Hargreaves") created the [Spinning Jenny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinning_Jenny "Spinning Jenny"), a device that could perform the work of a number of spinning wheels. However, while this invention could be operated by hand, the [water frame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_frame "Water frame"), invented by [Richard Arkwright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Arkwright "Richard Arkwright"), could be powered by a [water wheel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_wheel "Water wheel"). Indeed, Arkwright is credited with the widespread introduction of the [factory system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factory_system "Factory system") in Britain, and is the first example of the successful mill owner and industrialist in British history. The water frame was, however, soon supplanted by the [spinning mule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinning_mule "Spinning mule") (a cross between a water frame and a jenny) invented by [Samuel Crompton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Crompton "Samuel Crompton"). Mules were later constructed in iron by Messrs. Horrocks of Stockport.

As they were water powered, the first mills were constructed in rural locations by streams or rivers. Workers villages were created around them, such as [New Lanark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Lanark "New Lanark") Mills in Scotland. These spinning mills resulted in the decline of the [domestic system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_system "Domestic system"), in which spinning with old slow equipment was undertaken in rural cottages.

The [steam engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine "Steam engine") was invented and became a power supply that soon surpassed waterfalls and horsepower. The first practicable steam engine was invented by [Thomas Newcomen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Newcomen "Thomas Newcomen"), and was used for pumping water out of mines. A much more powerful steam engine was invented by [James Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Watt "James Watt"); it had a [reciprocating engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reciprocating_engine "Reciprocating engine") capable of powering machinery. The first steam-driven textile mills began to appear in the last quarter of the 18th century, and this transformed the industrial revolution into an urban phenomenon, greatly contributing to the appearance and rapid growth of industrial towns.

The progress of the textile trade soon outstripped the original supplies of raw materials. By the turn of the 19th century, imported American cotton had replaced wool in the [North West of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_of_England "North West of England"), though wool remained the chief textile in [Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire "Yorkshire"). Textiles have been identified as the catalyst in technological change in this period. The application of steam power stimulated the demand for coal; the demand for machinery and rails stimulated the [iron industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_industry "Iron industry"); and the demand for transportation to move raw material in and finished products out stimulated the growth of the canal system, and (after 1830) the railway system.

Such an unprecedented degree of economic growth was not sustained by domestic demand alone. The application of technology and the factory system created such levels of [mass production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_production "Mass production") and cost efficiency that enabled British manufacturers to export inexpensive cloth and other items worldwide.

[Walt Rostow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Rostow "Walt Rostow") has posited the 1790s as the "take-off" period for the industrial revolution. This means that a process previously responding to domestic and other external stimuli began to feed upon itself, and became an unstoppable and irreversible process of sustained industrial and technological expansion.

In the late 18th century and early 19th century a series of technological advances led to the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution "Industrial Revolution"). Britain's position as the world's pre-eminent trader helped fund research and experimentation. The nation also had some of the world's greatest reserves of [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal "Coal"), the main fuel of the new revolution.

It was also fuelled by a rejection of mercantilism in favour of the predominance of [Adam Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Smith "Adam Smith")'s [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism "Capitalism"). The fight against Mercantilism was led by a number of [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism "Liberalism") thinkers, such as [Richard Cobden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Cobden "Richard Cobden"), [Joseph Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Hume "Joseph Hume"), [Francis Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Place "Francis Place") and [John Roebuck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Roebuck "John Roebuck").

Some have stressed the importance of natural or financial resources that Britain received from its many overseas colonies or that profits from the British [slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery "History of slavery") between Africa and the Caribbean helped fuel industrial investment, citing "bigger markets for British goods, larger profits to British investors, more and cheaper raw materials for emerging industrial sectors, and more incentives for British consumers than were offered by domestic industries or other foreign markets".[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-55)

The Industrial Revolution saw a rapid transformation in the British economy and society. Previously, large industries had to be near forests or rivers for power. The use of coal-fuelled engines allowed them to be placed in large urban centres. These new factories proved far more efficient at producing goods than the [cottage industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cottage_industry "Cottage industry") of a previous era. These manufactured goods were sold around the world, and raw materials and luxury goods were imported to Britain.

### Empire

Main article: [Economy of the British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_British_Empire "Economy of the British Empire")

See also: [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire") and [Historiography of the British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_the_British_Empire "Historiography of the British Empire")

During the Industrial Revolution the empire became less important and less well-regarded. The British defeat in the [American War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_War_of_Independence "American War of Independence") (1775–1783) deprived it of its largest and most developed colonies. This loss brought a realisation that colonies were not particularly economically beneficial to the home economy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-56) It was realised that the costs of occupation of colonies often exceeded the financial return to the taxpayer. In other words, formal empire afforded no great economic benefit when trade would continue whether the overseas political entities were nominally sovereign or not. The American Revolution helped demonstrate this by showing that Britain could still control trade with the colonies without having to pay for their defence and governance. This encouraged the British to grant their colonies self-government, starting with [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Canada "History of Canada"), which became unified and largely independent in 1867, and [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Australia "History of Australia"), which followed suit in 1901.[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-57)

### Napoleonic Wars

See also: [French Revolutionary and Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolutionary_and_Napoleonic_Wars "French Revolutionary and Napoleonic Wars") and [Post-Napoleonic Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Napoleonic_Depression "Post-Napoleonic Depression")

Critical to British success in confronting Napoleon was its superior economic situation. It was able to mobilise the nation's industrial and financial resources and apply them to defeating the [First French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_French_Empire "First French Empire"). With a population of 16 million Britain was barely half the size of France with 30 million. In terms of soldiers, the French numerical advantage was offset by British subsidies that paid for a large proportion of the Austrian and Russian soldiers, peaking at about 450,000 in 1813.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-58)

Most important, the British national output remained strong. Textiles and iron grew sharply. Iron production expanded as demand for cannon and munitions was insatiable. Agricultural prices soared—it was a golden age for agriculture even as food shortages appeared here and there. There were riots in Cornwall, Devon, and Somerset during the food shortages of 1800–01. Mobs forced merchants to hand over their stocks, as the food was distributed to the hungry by popular committees. Wells concludes that the disturbances indicate deep social grievances that extended far beyond the immediate food shortages.[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-59) Overall, however, crop production grew 50% between 1795 and 1815.[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-60)

The system of smuggling finished products into the continent undermined French efforts to ruin the British economy by cutting off markets. The well-organised business sector channelled products into what the military needed. Not only did British cloth provide for British uniforms, it clothed the allies as well and indeed the French soldiers too. Britain used its economic power to expand the Royal Navy, doubling the number of frigates and increasing the number of large ships of the line by 50%, while increasing the roster of sailors from 15,000 to 133,000 in eight years after the war began in 1793. France, meanwhile, saw its navy shrink by more than half.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-61)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Dette_britannique_longue_p%C3%A9riode.png/500px-Dette_britannique_longue_p%C3%A9riode.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dette_britannique_longue_p%C3%A9riode.png) The [national debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_debt "National debt") of the United Kingdom was at a record high percentage of the GDP as the Napoleonic wars ended, but was largely repaid by 1914.

The British budget in 1814 reached £66 million, including £10 million for the Navy, £40 million for the Army, £10 million for the Allies, and £38 million as interest on the national debt. The national debt soared to £679 million, more than double the GDP. It was willingly supported by hundreds of thousands of investors and tax payers, despite the higher taxes on land and a new income tax.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-62) The whole cost of the war came to £831 million. By contrast the French financial system was inadequate and Napoleon's forces had to rely in part on requisitions from conquered lands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-63)[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-64)[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-65)

#### Long-term favourable impact

O'Brien examines the long-term economic impact of the wars of 1793–1815, and finds them generally favourable, except for damage to the working class. The economy was not damaged by the diversion of manpower to the army and navy; in terms of destruction and enforced transfer of national wealth, Britain came out ahead. British control of the oceans proved optimal in creating a liberal free-trade global economy, and helped Britain gain the lion's share of the world's carrying trade and financial support services. The effects were positive for agriculture and most industries, apart from construction. The rate of capital formation was slowed somewhat and national income perhaps would have grown even faster without war. The most negative impact was a drop in living standards for the urban working classes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-66)

## 19th century

See also: [Economy, industry, and trade of the Victorian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy,_industry,_and_trade_of_the_Victorian_era "Economy, industry, and trade of the Victorian era")

19th century Britain was the world's [richest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_total_wealth "List of countries by total wealth") and most advanced economy, while 19th century Ireland experienced the worst famine in Europe in that century.[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-67) Real GDP per person almost doubled in the 90 years between 1780 and 1870, when it reached $3263 per capita. This was one third greater than GDP per person in the United States, and 70% more than both France and Germany.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Nicholas_Crafts_Spring_1998-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-Nicholas_Crafts_Spring_1998) The economy was the most industrialised in the world, with one-third of the population employed in manufacturing by 1870 (concurrently one-sixth of the workforce in the United States was employed in manufacturing). The level of quantifiable steam power (in both industry and railroad travel), was gauged at 7,600 [hp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horsepower "Horsepower") in 1880, only exceeded by the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hobsbawm,_1975;_309-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-Hobsbawm,_1975;_309) Urbanisation was so intense that by 1901 80% of the British population lived in towns.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Morgan_2008;_474-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-Morgan_2008;_474) The number of towns with a population over 50,000 reached 32 between 1847 and 1850, double that of Germany and almost five times that of the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hobsbawm,_1975;_309-1)[\[68\]](#refnote-Hobsbawm,_1975;_309) By 1901 there were 74 British towns which met the 50,000 minimum threshold.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Morgan_2008;_474-1)[\[69\]](#refnote-Morgan_2008;_474)

### Free trade

Main article: [Eden Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eden_Agreement "Eden Agreement")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Droits_de_douane_%28France%2C_UK%2C_US%29.png/500px-Droits_de_douane_%28France%2C_UK%2C_US%29.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Droits_de_douane_(France,_UK,_US).png) Tariff Rates (France, UK, US)

[Free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade "Free trade") was intellectually established by 1780 and implemented in the 1840s, thanks to the unusually strong influence of political theorists such as [Adam Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Smith "Adam Smith"). They convincingly argued that the old policy of [mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism "Mercantilism") held back the British economy, which if unfettered was poised to dominate world trade. As predicted, British dominance of world trade was apparent by the 1850s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-71)

After 1840, Britain committed its economy to [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade "Free trade"), with few barriers or tariffs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-72) This was most evident in the repeal in 1846 of the [Corn Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Laws "Corn Laws"), which had imposed stiff tariffs on imported grain. The end of these laws opened the British market to unfettered competition; grain prices fell, and food became more plentiful within Great Britain. The same was not true, however, in Ireland where the 1840s saw the worst famine in Europe in that century. By re-introducing income taxes in 1842 at the rate of 7 pennies on the pound (or roughly 3%) for incomes over £150, the government of [Sir Robert Peel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Robert_Peel "Sir Robert Peel") was able to compensate for loss of revenue and repeal import duties on over 700 items.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-73)

From 1815 to 1870, Britain reaped the benefits of being the world's first modern, industrialised nation. It described itself as "the workshop of the world", meaning that its finished goods were produced so efficiently and cheaply that they could often undersell comparable, locally manufactured goods in almost any other market.[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-74) If political conditions in a particular overseas market were stable enough, Britain could dominate its economy through free trade alone without having to resort to formal rule or mercantilism. Britain was even supplying half the needs in manufactured goods of such nations as Germany, France, Belgium, and the United States. By 1820, 30% of Britain's exports went to [its Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire"), rising slowly to 35% by 1910.[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-75) Until the latter 19th century, India remained Britain's economic jewel in terms of both imports and exports. In 1867, when British exports to her Empire totaled £50 million, £21 million of that was earned from India's market alone.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lawrence-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-Lawrence) Second to India, but far behind, was Australia, whose imports from Britain totaled £8 million, followed by Canada (£5.8 million), Hong Kong (£2.5 million), Singapore (£2 million), and New Zealand (£1.6 million).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lawrence-1)[\[75\]](#refnote-Lawrence) While these figures were undoubtedly significant, they represented just over a third of total British exports, the same proportion as over forty years before.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lawrence-2)[\[75\]](#refnote-Lawrence)

Apart from coal, iron, tin and [kaolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaolin "Kaolin") most raw materials had to be imported so that, in the 1830s, the main imports were (in order): raw cotton (from the American South), sugar (from the West Indies), wool, silk, tea (from China), timber (from Canada), wine, flax, hides and tallow.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-77) By 1900, Britain's global share had soared to 22.8% of total imports. By 1922, its global share was 14.9% of total exports and 28.8% of manufactured exports.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-78)

However, while in the 1890s Britain persisted in its free trade policy its major rivals, the U.S. and Germany, turned to high and moderately high tariffs respectively. American heavy industry grew faster than Britain and by the 1890s was competing with British machinery and other products in the world market.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-79)

#### Imperialism of Free Trade

Main article: [The Imperialism of Free Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Imperialism_of_Free_Trade "The Imperialism of Free Trade")

Historians agree that in the 1840s, Britain adopted a free-trade policy, meaning open markets and no tariffs throughout the empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-80) The debate among historians involves what the implications of free trade actually were. " [The Imperialism of Free Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Imperialism_of_Free_Trade "The Imperialism of Free Trade")" is a highly influential 1952 article by [John Gallagher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Andrew_Gallagher "John Andrew Gallagher") and [Ronald Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Robinson "Ronald Robinson").[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-81)[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-82) They argued that the [New Imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Imperialism "New Imperialism") of the 1880s, especially the [Scramble for Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa "Scramble for Africa"), was a continuation of a long-term policy in which informal empire, based on the principles of free trade, was favoured over formal imperial control. The article helped launch the [Cambridge School of historiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_School_of_historiography "Cambridge School of historiography"). Gallagher and Robinson used the British experience to construct a framework for understanding European imperialism that swept away the [all-or-nothing thinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-or-nothing_thinking "All-or-nothing thinking") of previous historians.[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-83) They found that European leaders rejected the notion that "imperialism" had to be based upon formal, legal control by one government over a colonial region. Much more important was informal influence in independent areas. According to Wm. Roger Louis, "In their view, historians have been mesmerized by formal empire and maps of the world with regions colored red. The bulk of British emigration, trade, and capital went to areas outside the formal British Empire. Key to their thinking is the idea of empire 'informally if possible and formally if necessary.'"[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-84) Oron Hale says that Gallagher and Robinson looked at the British involvement in Africa where they, "found few capitalists, less capital, and not much pressure from the alleged traditional promoters of colonial expansion. Cabinet decisions to annex or not to annex were made, usually on the basis of political or geopolitical considerations."[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-85)

Reviewing the debate from the end of the 20th century, historian Martin Lynn argues that Gallagher and Robinson exaggerated the impact. He says that Britain achieved its goal of increasing its economic interests in many areas, "but the broader goal of 'regenerating' societies and thereby creating regions tied as 'tributaries' to British economic interests was not attained." The reasons were:

> the aim to reshape the world through free trade and its extension overseas owed more to the misplaced optimism of British policy-makers and their partial views of the world than to an understanding of the realities of the mid-19th century globe... the volumes of trade and investment ... the British were able to generate remained limited... Local economies and local regimes proved adept at restricting the reach of British trade and investment. Local impediments to foreign inroads, the inhabitants' low purchasing power, the resilience of local manufacturing, and the capabilities of local entrepreneurs meant that these areas effectively resisted British economic penetration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-86)

### Agriculture

Main article: [British Agricultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Agricultural_Revolution "British Agricultural Revolution")

Further information: [Agriculture in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_the_United_Kingdom "Agriculture in the United Kingdom")

A free market for imported foodstuffs, the driving factor behind the 1846 repeal of the Corn Laws, reaped long-term benefits for consumers in Great Britain as world agricultural production increased.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-87) Consumers in Ireland, where there was [severe famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_(Ireland) "Great Famine (Ireland)") in the 1840s, did not benefit to the same extent. At first agriculture in Great Britain, through its superior productivity, was able to weather and even thrive following the repeal of the Corn Laws, contrary to the dire warnings of the landowners who had warned of immediate agricultural ruin. By the 1870s, the global price of grain began to fall dramatically following the opening up of the [Midwestern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States "Midwestern United States") and interior of Canada to mechanised cultivation. Combined with lower global transportation costs, the average price of a [quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarter_(unit) "Quarter (unit)") of grain fell from 56 _s_ in the years 1867–71, to 27 _s_ 3 _d_ per quarter in 1894–98.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173)[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-89) This lowered the cost of living and enabled Britain to meet the demands of a quickly growing population (grain imports tripled in volume between 1870 and 1914, while the population grew 43% between 1871 and 1901).[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-90)[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-91)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173-1)[\[87\]](#refnote-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173) It also caused the [Great Depression of British Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression_of_British_Agriculture "Great Depression of British Agriculture") in the countryside by the late 1870s, where a series of bad harvests combined with the far cheaper price of foreign grain induced a long decline for the British agricultural sector. Wheat-producing areas like [East Anglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Anglia "East Anglia") were especially hard hit, with overall wheat cultivation down from 13% of agricultural output in 1870, to 4% in 1900.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173-2)[\[87\]](#refnote-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173) Landowners argued for a re-introduction of the Corn Laws to protect domestic farming, but this was rebuffed by Prime Minister [Benjamin Disraeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli "Benjamin Disraeli"), who argued that returning to protectionism would endanger British manufacturing supremacy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-92)

In addition to the general slump in demand, greater mechanisation in British agriculture, typified by the introduction of steam-powered [threshing machines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threshing_machine "Threshing machine"), mowers, and reapers, increased unemployment for rural workers. The result was an acceleration of migration from country to town, where jobs in factories, domestic service, and other occupations offered better wages and more opportunities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Morgan,_2008_p.479-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-Morgan,_2008_p.479) The male workforce of the countryside decreased by 40% between 1861 and 1901, while agriculture as a percentage of the national wealth fell from 20.3% in 1851 to just 6.4% by 1901.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Morgan,_2008_p.479-1)[\[92\]](#refnote-Morgan,_2008_p.479) The depression did not apply only to foodstuffs, but also to wool producers, a once vital sector undercut by a flood of cheap wool imports from Australia and New Zealand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173-3)[\[87\]](#refnote-Carter_&_Mears_2011_p._173) Only select types of produce where freshness was imperative, like milk and meat, enjoyed strong domestic demand in the late 19th century.

The declining profitability of agriculture in the latter decades of the 19th century left British landowners hard pressed to maintain their accustomed lifestyles. The connection between land ownership and wealth which had for centuries underpinned the [British aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_aristocracy "British aristocracy") began an inexorable decline. Rents fell some 26% between the mid-1870s and mid-1890s, just as the amount of land under cultivation fell some 19%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-94) 88% of British millionaires between the years 1809–1879 were defined as landowners; the proportion fell to 33% in the years 1880–1914, as a new class of [plutocrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutocrats "Plutocrats") emerged from industry and finance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-95)

### Recessions

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Peterloo_Massacre.png/500px-Peterloo_Massacre.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Peterloo_Massacre.png) The Peterloo Massacre of 1819

Britain's 19th century economic growth was beset by frequent and sometimes severe recessions. The [Post-Napoleonic depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Napoleonic_depression "Post-Napoleonic depression") following the end of the Wars in 1815 was induced by several years of poor harvests, which were aggravated by the [Corn Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Laws "Corn Laws") of 1815. This law set high tariffs on imported foodstuffs, keeping the cost of grain artificially high while wages were declining.[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-96)[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-97) As early as 1816, the high cost of grain caused famine and unrest in areas such as [East Anglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Anglia "East Anglia") and the North of England, where rioters seized grain stores and attacked the homes of suspected profiteers and merchants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-98) The high food prices caused an overall slump in consumption and consequently in industrial production and employment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-99) The discontent of the workers culminated in the disastrous confrontation with the authorities at the [Peterloo Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peterloo_Massacre "Peterloo Massacre") of 1819, when British cavalry rode headfirst into a crowd of 60,000 to 80,000 protesters in [Manchester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester "Manchester"), killing 5 and wounding as many as 700 people.
[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-100)[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-101)

The recession of the " [Hungry Forties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungry_Forties "Hungry Forties")" was similar in its nature to that of the 1820s. Like that of the years following 1815, the 1840s recession was caused by a series of bad harvests, this time from a blight affecting potatoes facilitated by unusually wet and cold conditions in Northern Europe. Ireland, where the population was heavily dependent on potatoes for subsistence, was the worst affected. On mainland Britain, regions of the [Scottish Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands "Scottish Highlands") and the [Outer Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Hebrides "Outer Hebrides") were worst affected by the potato blight (some parts were depopulated by as much as 50%).[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-102) The [Corn Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Laws "Corn Laws") inhibited the ability of the British government to import food for the starving in Ireland and Scotland, which led the Tory Prime Minister [Sir Robert Peel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Robert_Peel "Sir Robert Peel") to defy the landed interests in Parliament and force the abolition of the [Corn Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Laws "Corn Laws") in June 1846. The abolition was only accomplished in phases through 1849, by which time Ireland and the Highlands had lost much of their populations to famine or emigration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-103) Relief funding by the British government for the famine in Ireland was dramatically cut in 1847 because of the financial crisis that year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-104) Many blamed the crisis on Peel's wider economic policy but the repeal of the [Corn Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Laws "Corn Laws"), combined with the astronomic growth of the railways, served to lift Britain out of recession in the 1850s, providing the basis for steady growth in population and output over the next few decades.[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-105)

### Railways

Main article: [History of rail transport in Great Britain 1830–1922](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_rail_transport_in_Great_Britain_1830%E2%80%931922 "History of rail transport in Great Britain 1830–1922")

The British invented the modern railway system and exported it to the world. This emerged from Britain's elaborate system of canals and roadways, which both used horses to haul coal. Domestic consumption in household heaths remained an important market though coal also fired the new steam engines installed in textile factories. Britain furthermore had the engineers and entrepreneurs needed to create and finance a railway system. In 1815, [George Stephenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stephenson "George Stephenson") invented the modern steam locomotive, launching a technological race: bigger, more powerful locomotives using higher and higher steam pressures. Stephenson's key innovation came when he integrated all the components of a railways _system_ in 1825 by opening the [Stockton and Darlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockton_and_Darlington "Stockton and Darlington") line. It demonstrated it was commercially feasible to have a system of usable length. London poured money into railway building—a veritable bubble, but one with permanent value.

[Thomas Brassey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Brassey "Thomas Brassey") brought British railway engineering to the world, with construction crews that in the 1840s employed 75,000 men across Europe. Every nation copied the British model. Brassey expanded throughout the British Empire and Latin America. His companies invented and improved thousands of mechanical devices, and developed the science of civil engineering to build roadways, tunnels and bridges.

The [telegraph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_telegraphy_in_the_United_Kingdom "Electrical telegraphy in the United Kingdom"), although invented and developed separately, proved essential for the internal communications of the railways. They allowed slower trains to pull over as express trains raced through; made it possible to use a single track for two-way traffic, and to locate where repairs were needed.

In the early period, recognition of the potential of the railways along with led to a period of speculation and investment referred to as [Railway Mania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_Mania "Railway Mania"). The boom years were 1836 and 1845–47, when Parliament authorised 8,000 miles of railways with a projected future total of £200 million; that about equalled one year of Britain's GDP. Once a charter was obtained, there was little government regulation, as laissez faire and private ownership had become accepted practices. Britain had a superior financial system based in London that funded both the railways in Britain and also in many other parts of the world, including the United States, up until 1914.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/68/George_Hudson_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17293.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:George_Hudson_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17293.jpg) [George Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Hudson "George Hudson")

[Isambard Kingdom Brunel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isambard_Kingdom_Brunel "Isambard Kingdom Brunel") (1806–1859) designed the first major railway, the [Great Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Western_Railway "Great Western Railway"), built originally in the 1830s to cover the 100 miles from London to Bristol. Even more important was the highly controversial [George Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Hudson "George Hudson"). He became Britain's "railway king" by merging numerous short lines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-106) Since there was no government agency supervising the railways, Hudson set up a system that all the lines adopted called the [Railway Clearing House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_Clearing_House "Railway Clearing House"). It made interconnections easy for people and freight by standardising routines for transferring freight and people between companies, and loaning out freight cars. By 1849 Hudson controlled nearly 30% of Britain's trackage. Hudson did away with accountants and manipulated funds—paying large dividends out of capital because profits were quite low, but no one knew that until his system collapsed and the railway bubble of the late 1840s burst.[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-107)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Euston_Station_showing_wrought_iron_roof_of_1837.jpg/500px-Euston_Station_showing_wrought_iron_roof_of_1837.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Euston_Station_showing_wrought_iron_roof_of_1837.jpg) [Euston station in London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euston_railway_station "Euston railway station"), 1837\. Note the open passenger carriages.

By 1850 Britain had a well integrated, well engineered system that provided fast, on-time, inexpensive movement of freight and people to every city and most rural districts. Freight rates had plunged to a penny a ton mile for coal. The system directly or indirectly employed tens of thousands of engineers, conductors, mechanics, repairmen, accountants, station agents and managers, bringing a new level of business sophistication that could be applied to many other industries, and helping many small and large businesses to expand their role in the industrial revolution. Thus railways had a tremendous impact on industrialisation. By lowering transportation costs, they reduced costs for all industries moving supplies and finished goods, and they increased demand for the production of all the inputs needed for the railway system itself. The system kept growing; by 1880, there were 13,500 locomotives which each carried 97,800 passengers a year, or 31,500 tons of freight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-108)

### Second Industrial Revolution

Main article: [Second Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Industrial_Revolution "Second Industrial Revolution")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Graph_rel_share_world_manuf_1750_1900_02.png/500px-Graph_rel_share_world_manuf_1750_1900_02.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Graph_rel_share_world_manuf_1750_1900_02.png)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/6f/Graph_rel_lvl_indz_1750_1900_01.png/500px-Graph_rel_lvl_indz_1750_1900_01.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Graph_rel_lvl_indz_1750_1900_01.png)

During the First Industrial Revolution, the industrialist replaced the merchant as the dominant figure in the capitalist system. In the later decades of the 19th century, when the ultimate control and direction of large industry came into the hands of financiers, [industrial capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_capitalism "Industrial capitalism") gave way to [financial capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_capitalism "Financial capitalism") and the corporation. The establishment of behemoth industrial empires, whose assets were controlled and managed by men divorced from production, was a dominant feature of this third phase. By the middle of the 19th century, as the world's only fully industrialised nation, British output represented just under half the total of the world's industrial capacity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lawrence-3)[\[75\]](#refnote-Lawrence)

New products and services were also introduced which greatly increased international trade. Improvements in [steam engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine "Steam engine") design and the wide availability of cheap iron (and after 1870 steel) meant that slow, sailing ships could be replaced with [steamships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamship "Steamship"), such as [Brunel's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isambard_Kingdom_Brunel "Isambard Kingdom Brunel") _[SS Great Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS_Great_Western "SS Great Western")_. Electricity and [chemical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_chemical_engineering "History of chemical engineering") industries became important although Britain lagged behind the U.S. and Germany.

Amalgamation of industrial [cartels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartel "Cartel") into larger corporations, mergers and alliances of separate firms, and technological advancement (particularly the increased use of electric power and [internal combustion engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_combustion_engine "Internal combustion engine") fuelled by petrol) were mixed blessings for British business during the late [Victorian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_era "Victorian era"). The ensuing development of more intricate and efficient machines along with mass production techniques greatly expanded output and lowered production costs. As a result, production often exceeded domestic demand.
